I don't get this... this feeling of incompleteness you're talking about, unless, as I have been trying to emphasize, it involves the stove, the front door, or washing my hands after dealing with this infernal wrist brace. Those are the only times I get that awful, nagging sensation that something isn't right. If I'm just, say, folding my laundry or buttering my toast, I just do it and I'm done. There's no agonizing over whether the butter is spread with perfect, mathematical evenness across the bread. \n\nSo, to be perfectly clear—and I do hope we can perhaps move on to discussing how to actually stop these specific routines that are making me late for the museum—no, I do not have a general problem with needing things to be exact or precise just for the sake of it. It's really just the checking and the washing. That's the entirety of it.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what happens in your mind when these intrusive thoughts come—do they feel like something you fear might happen, or more like something you simply can't ignore even though you know it's not reasonable?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... to answer your question directly—though I’ve found it’s never quite a simple 'this or that' when it comes to the human mind, is it?—it is very much the latter. It is the utter unreasonableness of it all that is so completely maddening to me. \n\nPicture this, if you will. I will be sitting quietly in my study, perfectly at peace, reading a new historical text—I'm currently making my way through a rather dense but entirely fascinating volume on the Reconstruction era, which requires a good deal of focus—or perhaps I'm just gathering my coat, my keys, and my notes, getting ready to head out for my cataloging shift at the museum. I'm going about my business, entirely rational, completely grounded in reality. \n\nAnd then, entirely uninvited, this... this ugly little thought just barges right into my consciousness. It doesn't knock, it just intrudes, completely interrupting my train of thought. And it's always this nagging, persistent whisper that I’ve left something unsafe, like a burner on the stove that might catch a stray tea towel on fire, or that I’ve touched something terribly unclean when adjusting this wretched wrist brace of mine, or—and this is perhaps the most bothersome variation—that through some minor negligence on my part, I have somehow put someone else at risk. \n\nNow, do I genuinely, logically fear that the house is going to burst into flames or that I've contracted some deadly plague from my own wrist brace? No, of course not. I know perfectly well that the thought is entirely out of proportion to reality. I am a rational man, doctor. I spent decades teaching graduate students how to critically evaluate evidence, for heaven's sake! I know the stove is off because I haven't even cooked anything on it since yesterday evening. \n\nBut the thought just sits there, squatting right in the middle of my mind, and I simply cannot ignore it. It demands my attention. It's like a fly buzzing directly inside your ear; you know perfectly well the fly poses no actual threat to your life, but you cannot possibly focus on your book or walk out the door until you've dealt with it. The thought itself isn't a profound terror, it's just an impossible, immovable obstacle. And so, I have to go check the burner, or wash my hands again, just to clear the air, so to speak, even though I know—with every fiber of my intellect—that it is completely ridiculous.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other specific thoughts or actions, even minor ones, that you feel you must repeat or dwell on to relieve discomfort or a sense of things being incomplete?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I truly believe we've mapped the entirety of this particular territory, doctor. I mean, if there were other things, I certainly would have noticed them by now, given how much time I spend analyzing my own behavior lately. But no, there really aren't any other hidden habits or minor rituals lurking in the background. \n\nReally, what brings me in here to see you—the entire crux of the matter, if you will—is simply that I’ve gotten terribly stuck doing these specific little routines we've been discussing. It’s the washing of my hands over and over again after I have to handle this wretched wrist brace. It’s the endless rechecking of the stove and the front door, standing there staring at the deadbolt. And, of course, that silent counting I do through the steps before I can finally manage to leave the porch. \n\nThat is the sum total of it. But the reason it has become such an intolerable issue—and why I finally swallowed my pride and made this appointment—is that these repetitions are starting to make me genuinely late for my cataloging shifts at the museum. And I have never been a late person in my entire life! Punctuality is a virtue I hold quite dear, both in myself and in others, so you can imagine how incredibly frustrating it is to be standing there on my own front porch, looking at my watch, knowing that I am going to be tardy, all because I am stuck in this ridiculous loop. It is absolutely maddening because I know—I know with absolute, logical certainty—that I’ve already done enough. I know the door is locked, I know the stove is off, I know my hands are perfectly clean, but I just get stuck anyway. So, no, there isn't anything else. Just those specific hurdles I can't seem to get over.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to arrange objects in a specific order, make sure things are symmetrical, or count items repeatedly, even if it's not part of the routine you described with the front door or stove?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... well, I must confess I feel a bit like I'm repeating my own lecture here. We seem to be covering the exact same ground I just laid out for you. \n\nTo answer your question about symmetry and arranging—no. I mean, certainly, if you were to look at my workspace down in the archives, you would see a very tidy desk. I organize my historical documents chronologically, and I prefer my reference materials stacked neatly, but that is simply because it is an efficient way to work. If a colleague bumps my desk and my notepad shifts an inch to the left, I don't feel any burning, uncomfortable need to perfectly realign it to the edge of the table. It just doesn't bother me in the slightest. \n\nAnd as for counting other things... no, absolutely not. I don't count the books on a shelf, or the tiles on the floor, or the number of times I stir my tea. As I quite literally just finished explaining a moment ago, what brings me in here—the entire reason we are speaking today—is simply that I’ve gotten terribly stuck doing *these specific* little routines. It is the washing of my hands again after handling my wrist brace. It is the rechecking of the stove and the front door. And it is that silent counting I do through the steps before I leave the porch. But that is the *only* counting I do! \n\nAnd the whole reason it has become such a burden is that they’re starting to make me late for my shifts, even when I know perfectly well that I’ve already done enough. I know the door is locked, I know my hands are clean, but I get stuck anyway. That is the entire scope of the issue. There isn't any other arranging or symmetrical nonsense going on, I assure you.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Doctor, I... I must admit, I am finding this line of questioning incredibly circular. We are, quite frankly, beating a dead horse at this point, and I feel a bit as though I am standing at a lectern trying to get a rather inattentive freshman seminar to grasp a fundamental concept. 

Let me be as unequivocally clear as I can be, because it seems you are searching for some sprawling, generalized neurosis that simply does not exist. The answer is no, not in other situations. It is exactly, and only, what I have already laid out for you. 

I’ll be sitting peacefully, reading at home, minding my own business, or I'll be in the foyer getting ready for a museum shift... and that is when it happens. That ugly little thought keeps barging in, completely uninvited, insisting that I’ve left something unsafe, or that I’ve touched something terribly unclean, or that I have somehow put someone at risk. 

It doesn't happen when I'm at the grocer, it doesn't happen when I'm taking a stroll through the park, and it doesn't happen when I'm actually at the museum cataloging documents. It happens right there, in those specific instances I just spent the better part of this appointment describing to you! And, as I have emphasized repeatedly, I know perfectly well it’s completely out of proportion. My faculties are entirely intact, Doctor. I know the thoughts are irrational. 

So, no, there are no other situations, not even mildly. Can we perhaps accept that this is the full scope of the intrusion and finally move on to what we might actually do about it? Because this endless repetition of the same question is precisely the sort of inefficiency that drives me up the wall.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F42.0, F42.1, F42.8, F42.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, thank you for seeing me. I suppose what brings me in is... well, it’s these routines I’ve gotten myself stuck in lately. It’s hard to explain, but I just find myself doing these little things over and over. For instance, I wear this wrist brace—carpal tunnel from years of typing up reports before I retired—and every time I touch it or adjust it, I just have to wash my hands. And I mean, I know it’s my own brace, it’s not dirty, but I just can’t seem to stop myself from going to the sink. \n\nAnd it’s not just the washing. It’s getting out of the house, too. I’ll check the stove—make sure all the dials are off, you know—and then I’ll check the front door to make sure it’s locked. But then I’ll walk down the steps, and I have this... this counting thing I do in my head. I have to silently count through the steps before I leave, and if I lose track or it doesn’t feel right, I have to go back up and check the door again, and sometimes the stove again, too. \n\nIt’s just... it’s starting to make me late for things. The other day I was supposed to meet a former colleague for coffee, and I was nearly twenty minutes late because I just couldn’t get past the front porch without going back in. I know I’ve already checked everything, I know I’ve done enough, but my brain just won’t let it go until I do the whole routine perfectly. It’s very frustrating, to be honest. I just want to be able to leave the house like a normal person without all this fuss.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself washing your hands or checking the stove and door, are you having troubling thoughts or images in your mind that you can’t seem to get rid of—like a fear something bad will happen if you don’t do the routine perfectly?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, exactly. It’s... it’s like this constant buzzing in the back of my mind. For example, I volunteer at the historical museum downtown—just a couple of afternoons a week, mostly to get out of the house and put my degree to some use, you know, rather than just sitting around reading all day. And I’ll be sitting in my armchair at home, perfectly comfortable, maybe reading a biography or something, or I’ll be getting my coat on to head down to the museum for my shift, and suddenly this... this ugly little thought just barges right in. \n\nIt’s completely uninvited, of course. It’ll just pop into my head that I’ve left the stove on and the whole house is going to catch fire, or that when I adjusted my wrist brace I touched something horribly unclean and now I’m going to spread germs all over the museum exhibits or get one of the visitors sick. Or that by not locking the door properly, I’m somehow putting my neighbors at risk if someone breaks in. And the ridiculous thing is, I know perfectly well it’s blown entirely out of proportion. I mean, I’m an educated man, I have a master's degree, I know logically that adjusting a clean wrist brace in my own home isn’t going to start a plague. But the thought is just so persistent, so loud, that I can’t focus on my book or enjoy my walk to the museum until I go back and do the routine to quiet it down. It’s just this nagging, awful feeling that I’ve left something unsafe or put someone at risk, and the only way to make it stop is to wash my hands again or check the locks one more time.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts cause you a lot of anxiety or distress, especially because they seem to go against what you know is reasonable or realistic?\n\nPatient: Well, I’m not entirely sure I would use the words \"a lot of anxiety,\" or at least, not if you mean it in the sense of having a panic attack or sitting there trembling. I don't get like that. I'm not... terrified, if that makes sense. It’s more of a... well, a profound irritation, I suppose you could say. A relentless sort of nuisance. \n\nBecause, exactly as you pointed out, it completely defies logic, and I think that is precisely what bothers me the most about the whole ordeal. Take the other evening, for instance. I was sitting in my favorite leather chair—the one right by the bay window in my study, it gets the absolute best evening light—and I was reading this incredibly detailed biography of Ulysses S. Grant. Fascinating man, really, much more complex than the standard history textbooks usually give him credit for. Anyway, I’m right in the middle of a chapter about the Vicksburg campaign, completely engrossed in the strategy of it all, and suddenly, there it is. That nagging little thought about the front door being unlocked just slides right into my head. \n\nNow, does it cause me distress? I don't know if I'd call it distress, but it certainly ruins a good evening! I try to just keep reading. I tell myself, 'You locked it when you brought the mail in at three o'clock, you know you did.' I mean, I spent my entire career writing comprehensive analytical reports, evaluating data, making logical deductions. My whole professional life—not to mention my graduate studies—was built entirely on rational thinking and evidence. So to sit there, knowing as a factual certainty that the door is secure, and yet feeling this... this absolute, undeniable insistence in my gut that I have to go check it anyway... it’s just maddening. \n\nIt’s incredibly frustrating to have your own brain hijack your evening like that, especially when you know better. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm deeply anxious or scared something catastrophic is actually happening in that moment, it’s just this persistent, annoying itch that I can’t seem to ignore, no matter how much I try to apply my own common sense to it. I just eventually have to put the book down, mark my page, and go check the deadbolt just to get some peace and quiet in my own head.\n\nDoctor: When you have these intrusive thoughts, do you feel a strong need to perform the washing or checking behaviors in a specific way or a certain number of times to make the thought go away or to feel at ease?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, exactly. As I was sort of touching on when we first started talking—and I suppose this really gets to the heart of what actually brings me in today—I’ve just gotten completely stuck doing these little routines, and they absolutely have to be done in a very specific manner. It’s not simply a matter of casually glancing at the stove or running my hands quickly under the tap. Oh, no, that would be far too easy, wouldn't it? \n\nTake the hand washing after I adjust my wrist brace, which I mentioned earlier. If I just rinse my hands, it doesn’t... it doesn’t settle that nagging itch in my brain. I have to use the soap, and I have to lather it up until it feels completely thorough, and if I happen to bump my knuckle against the edge of the sink while I'm rinsing—which happens more often than you'd think in my downstairs half-bath, it’s a very narrow pedestal sink that was installed back in the nineties—well, then the whole process is ruined. It doesn't feel right, and I have to wash them all over again from the beginning. \n\nAnd it’s the exact same story with the checking and the counting. Like I was telling you about the front door and the steps... I have to silently count through the steps in a very specific rhythm before I can actually leave the property. One, two, three, four... matching the cadence of my feet perfectly. If a neighbor waves at me, or a loud car drives by and distracts my train of thought, I lose the count. And if I lose the count, the whole departure is compromised. So I have to turn right around, march back up the steps, recheck the front door to make sure it's secure, usually go back inside and recheck the stove just to be absolutely certain, and start the whole counting process over again. \n\nIt’s just... well, it’s exactly why I made this appointment. These routines, having to do them in such a rigid, specific way just to feel at ease... they’re starting to make me constantly late for things. Even when I am standing there on the sidewalk, fully aware as a rational human being that I have already checked the dials and I have already locked the deadbolt and I know I’ve already done enough, I just can't walk away until the routine is executed perfectly. It’s a tremendous waste of time, really, but I just get entirely stuck.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other behaviors or thoughts that you feel compelled to repeat or dwell on, even if they seem unnecessary or excessive to you?\n\nPatient: Hmm. Well, let me just run through my day in my head... no, I really don't believe so. I like to think I’m a fairly self-aware person. You know, after spending my entire career writing those analytical reports I mentioned, and going through graduate school before that, you develop a rather keen eye for patterns and anomalies, especially in your own behavior. \n\nSince I retired, my daily routine is quite established, quite predictable, really. If I'm doing anything else—say, locking my car door at the grocery store, or turning off the television, or tying my shoes—I just do it once, my brain accepts that the task is complete, and I move on with my life exactly the way a rational person should. Frankly, the things we've already discussed are quite enough of a time-waster on their own! I'd much rather spend my afternoons down at the historical society cataloging the new donations than sitting around my house dwelling on things I already know perfectly well are fine.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other specific actions or thoughts that you feel you must repeat, even if only occasionally, to feel a sense of control or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I feel like we might be going around in circles just a bit here, because as I was just trying to explain a moment ago, there really isn't anything else hiding in the background. It’s exactly what I was just saying: washing my hands again and again after handling my wrist brace, the endless rechecking of the stove and the front door, and that silent counting through the steps before I can finally manage to leave the property. \n\nI don't do them to prevent some sort of catastrophic meteor strike or anything dramatic like that, it's just... well, I get stuck. It's more of an overwhelming, magnetic pull, a feeling that I simply *must* complete the cycle. \n\nYou see, as I've been trying to impress upon you—and really, this is exactly what brings me in to see you today, the whole core of the issue—is that I’ve just gotten completely, utterly stuck doing these little routines. When I'm standing there washing my hands again and again after handling my wrist brace, or doing that ridiculous rechecking of the stove and the front door, or silently counting through the steps before I can finally leave the property... it's because my brain simply will not let me walk away until it feels right. If I try to just force myself to walk away, that discomfort you mentioned flares up terribly. So I do it because I feel I absolutely must, just to get rid of that awful 'stuck' feeling so I can finally get on with my day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that things need to be arranged in a certain way, or that actions need to be done a certain number of times, not necessarily to prevent something bad from happening, but simply because it feels uncomfortable or 'not quite right' unless you do them that way?\n\nPatient: Arranged in a certain way? Like I was telling you about the front door and the steps... I have to silently count through the steps in a very specific rhythm before I can actually leave the property. One, two, three, four... matching the cadence of my feet perfectly. If a neighbor waves at me, or a loud car drives by and distracts my train of thought, I lose the count. And if I lose the count, the whole departure is compromised. So I have to turn right around, march back up the steps, recheck the front door to make sure it's secure, usually go back inside and recheck the stove just to be absolutely certain, and start the whole counting process over again. \n\nIt’s just... well, it’s exactly why I made this appointment. These routines, having to do them in such a rigid, specific way just to feel at ease... they’re starting to make me constantly late for things. Even when I am standing there on the sidewalk, fully aware as a rational human being that I have already checked the dials and I have already locked the deadbolt and I know I’ve already done enough, I just can't walk away until the routine is executed perfectly. It’s a tremendous waste of time, really, but I just get entirely stuck.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other behaviors or thoughts that you feel compelled to repeat or dwell on, even if they seem unnecessary or excessive to you?\n\nPatient: Hmm. Well, let me just run through my day in my head... no, I really don't believe so. I like to think I’m a fairly self-aware person. You know, after spending my entire career writing those analytical reports I mentioned, and going through graduate school before that, you develop a rather keen eye for patterns and anomalies, especially in your own behavior. \n\nSince I retired, my daily routine is quite established, quite predictable, really. I wake up, I make my coffee—French press, always, I find those automatic drip machines just completely ruin the flavor profile of a good dark roast—and I sit in the study to read the morning paper before I even think about heading down to the museum. And throughout all of that normal daily business, no, I haven't found myself getting stuck on anything else. \n\nIt really is just those specific things I’ve already detailed for you. The stove, the deadbolt on the front door, the hand washing specifically when the wrist brace is involved, and the counting on the front steps. That little collection of nuisances seems to be the absolute extent of it. If I'm doing anything else—say, locking my car door at the grocery store, or turning off the television, or tying my shoes—I just do it once, my brain accepts that the task is complete, and I move on with my life exactly the way a rational person should. There's none of that... that irritating, magnetic pull to go back and do it again. \n\nSo, no, I would have to say quite confidently that there isn't anything else hiding in the wings. Frankly, the things we've already discussed are quite enough of a time-waster on their own! I'd much rather spend my afternoons down at the historical society cataloging the new donations than sitting around my house dwelling on things I already know perfectly well are fine.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other specific actions or thoughts that you feel you must repeat, even if only occasionally, to feel a sense of control or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I feel like we might be going around in circles just a bit here, because as I was just trying to explain a moment ago, there really isn't anything else hiding in the background. I don't have a whole laundry list of other occasional habits, because frankly, the ones we've discussed take up quite enough of my time as it is! \n\nI mean, the entire reason I picked up the telephone and made this appointment in the first place—what actually brings me in today—is precisely that I’ve gotten completely stuck doing these specific little routines. It’s exactly what I was just saying: washing my hands again and again after handling my wrist brace, the endless rechecking of the stove and the front door, and that silent counting through the steps before I can finally manage to leave the property. \n\nI don't do them to prevent some sort of catastrophic meteor strike or anything dramatic like that, it's just... well, I get stuck. And the crux of the issue, the real problem here, is that they’re starting to make me genuinely late for my commitments, like my cataloging work down at the museum. And that is incredibly frustrating for someone who has always prided himself on absolute punctuality. I'll be standing there on the porch, running late, knowing full well—with absolute, rational certainty—that I’ve already done enough. I know the stove is off, I know the door is locked, I know my hands are clean. I know it. But I just get stuck in the loop of it anyway. So, to answer your question, no, there aren't any other actions. Just those ones, but I assure you, they are quite enough of a nuisance on their own to warrant me sitting in this chair today.\n\nDoctor: When you perform these washing or checking behaviors, is it primarily to reduce a feeling of discomfort or distress that comes with the intrusive thoughts, or do you do them simply because you feel you must, even if it's not particularly distressing?\n\nPatient: Distress... hmm. Well, I suppose \"distress\" might be a slightly dramatic word for it, at least in the traditional, cinematic sense. I’m not standing there weeping or hyperventilating, if that’s what you mean. But there is absolutely a profound, nagging discomfort. It's more of an overwhelming, magnetic pull, a feeling that I simply *must* complete the cycle. \n\nYou see, as I've been trying to impress upon you—and really, this is exactly what brings me in to see you today, the whole core of the issue—is that I’ve just gotten completely, utterly stuck doing these little routines. When I'm standing there washing my hands again and again after handling my wrist brace, or doing that ridiculous rechecking of the stove and the front door, or silently counting through the steps before I can finally leave the property... it's because my brain simply will not let me walk away until it feels right. If I try to just force myself to walk away, that discomfort you mentioned flares up terribly. It's like a mental itch that I can't scratch, a sense of incompleteness that just blocks out everything else.\n\nAnd the real irony of it all, the part that actually *does* cause me genuine distress, is that these routines are starting to make me terribly late for my commitments! I will literally be standing there on the porch, staring at my wristwatch, fully aware that I am going to be tardy for my cataloging work at the historical society, and knowing—with complete, rational certainty—that I’ve already done enough. I know the stove is off. I know the door is locked. I know my hands are perfectly clean. But I just get entirely stuck. I can't leave until the routine is satisfied. So I do it because I feel I absolutely must, just to get rid of that awful 'stuck' feeling so I can finally get on with my day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that things need to be arranged in a certain way, or that actions need to be done a certain number of times, not necessarily to prevent something bad from happening, but simply because it feels uncomfortable or 'not quite right' unless you do them that way?\n\nPatient: Arranged in a certain way? Like... symmetry, or lining things up perfectly? No, no, not at all. I mean, as I mentioned, my daily routine is quite established, but I don't have any particular compulsions about how the physical objects in my house are arranged. My books are organized by subject and then chronologically, of course, because that's simply the logical way to catalog a personal library—especially when you have as many historical texts as I do—but I don't feel any sort of agonizing discomfort if a spine is slightly out of alignment or if a pen is sitting crooked on my desk. \n\nNow, as for doing things a certain number of times... well, I suppose we've already covered that, haven't we? The counting on the front steps. But again, that's not about making sure I step exactly four times on each brick because it \"feels right.\" It's just part of that same agonizing loop I keep trying to explain. I get to the steps, and my brain says, \"Are you sure you checked the deadbolt? Are you sure?\" And then I'm stuck. The counting is just... it's just what happens while I'm stuck there, trying to break the magnetic pull to go back and check the lock for the fifth time. \n\nBut outside of that specific scenario—the door, the stove, the handwashing with the brace—no. I don't have to tap my coffee mug three times before I take a sip, or turn the light switch on and off until it feels perfectly satisfying. Honestly, doctor, if I had to deal with that on top of the things I'm already dealing with, I don't think I'd ever make it out of the house! It's just those specific routines that are causing the problem. Everything else in my life, I just do it once, exactly the way I always have, and move on.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a feeling that things need to be done in a very exact or precise way, not necessarily to prevent something bad from happening, but just because it feels uncomfortable or incomplete otherwise?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I... I feel like we are rather circling the drain on this particular nuance, aren't we? I mean, you keep asking about things needing to be exact or precise, or arranged just so, or done a certain number of times, and I... well, I really don't know how else to put it other than what I've just finished saying. No. \n\nI don't have this overarching need for absolute precision in every little thing I do. I mean, yes, I am a precise person by nature. You don't spend thirty-five years teaching history and another ten volunteering in an archive without developing a certain appreciation for accuracy. When I write a note, I expect my handwriting to be legible. When I make my tea in the morning, I let it steep for exactly four minutes because, well, that's how you make a proper cup of Earl Grey, not because my brain will short-circuit if I pull the bag out at three minutes and fifty seconds. \n\nIt's just not like that. I don't get this... this feeling of incompleteness you're talking about, unless, as I have been trying to emphasize, it involves the stove, the front door, or washing my hands after dealing with this infernal wrist brace. Those are the only times I get that awful, nagging sensation that something isn't right. If I'm just, say, folding my laundry or buttering my toast, I just do it and I'm done. There's no agonizing over whether the butter is spread with perfect, mathematical evenness across the bread. \n\nSo, to be perfectly clear—and I do hope we can perhaps move on to discussing how to actually stop these specific routines that are making me late for the museum—no, I do not have a general problem with needing things to be exact or precise just for the sake of it. It's really just the checking and the washing. That's the entirety of it.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what happens in your mind when these intrusive thoughts come—do they feel like something you fear might happen, or more like something you simply can't ignore even though you know it's not reasonable?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... to answer your question directly—though I’ve found it’s never quite a simple 'this or that' when it comes to the human mind, is it?—it is very much the latter. It is the utter unreasonableness of it all that is so completely maddening to me. \n\nPicture this, if you will. I will be sitting quietly in my study, perfectly at peace, reading a new historical text—I'm currently making my way through a rather dense but entirely fascinating volume on the Reconstruction era, which requires a good deal of focus—or perhaps I'm just gathering my coat, my keys, and my notes, getting ready to head out for my cataloging shift at the museum. I'm going about my business, entirely rational, completely grounded in reality. \n\nAnd then, entirely uninvited, this... this ugly little thought just barges right into my consciousness. It doesn't knock, it just intrudes, completely interrupting my train of thought. And it's always this nagging, persistent whisper that I’ve left something unsafe, like a burner on the stove that might catch a stray tea towel on fire, or that I’ve touched something terribly unclean when adjusting this wretched wrist brace of mine, or—and this is perhaps the most bothersome variation—that through some minor negligence on my part, I have somehow put someone else at risk. \n\nNow, do I genuinely, logically fear that the house is going to burst into flames or that I've contracted some deadly plague from my own wrist brace? No, of course not. I know perfectly well that the thought is entirely out of proportion to reality. I am a rational man, doctor. I spent decades teaching graduate students how to critically evaluate evidence, for heaven's sake! I know the stove is off because I haven't even cooked anything on it since yesterday evening. \n\nBut the thought just sits there, squatting right in the middle of my mind, and I simply cannot ignore it. It demands my attention. It's like a fly buzzing directly inside your ear; you know perfectly well the fly poses no actual threat to your life, but you cannot possibly focus on your book or walk out the door until you've dealt with it. The thought itself isn't a profound terror, it's just an impossible, immovable obstacle. And so, I have to go check the burner, or wash my hands again, just to clear the air, so to speak, even though I know—with every fiber of my intellect—that it is completely ridiculous.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other specific thoughts or actions, even minor ones, that you feel you must repeat or dwell on to relieve discomfort or a sense of things being incomplete?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I truly believe we've mapped the entirety of this particular territory, doctor. I mean, if there were other things, I certainly would have noticed them by now, given how much time I spend analyzing my own behavior lately. But no, there really aren't any other hidden habits or minor rituals lurking in the background. \n\nReally, what brings me in here to see you—the entire crux of the matter, if you will—is simply that I’ve gotten terribly stuck doing these specific little routines we've been discussing. It’s the washing of my hands over and over again after I have to handle this wretched wrist brace. It’s the endless rechecking of the stove and the front door, standing there staring at the deadbolt. And, of course, that silent counting I do through the steps before I can finally manage to leave the porch. \n\nThat is the sum total of it. But the reason it has become such an intolerable issue—and why I finally swallowed my pride and made this appointment—is that these repetitions are starting to make me genuinely late for my cataloging shifts at the museum. And I have never been a late person in my entire life! Punctuality is a virtue I hold quite dear, both in myself and in others, so you can imagine how incredibly frustrating it is to be standing there on my own front porch, looking at my watch, knowing that I am going to be tardy, all because I am stuck in this ridiculous loop. It is absolutely maddening because I know—I know with absolute, logical certainty—that I’ve already done enough. I know the door is locked, I know the stove is off, I know my hands are perfectly clean, but I just get stuck anyway. So, no, there isn't anything else. Just those specific hurdles I can't seem to get over.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to arrange objects in a specific order, make sure things are symmetrical, or count items repeatedly, even if it's not part of the routine you described with the front door or stove?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... well, I must confess I feel a bit like I'm repeating my own lecture here. We seem to be covering the exact same ground I just laid out for you. \n\nTo answer your question about symmetry and arranging—no. I mean, certainly, if you were to look at my workspace down in the archives, you would see a very tidy desk. I organize my historical documents chronologically, and I prefer my reference materials stacked neatly, but that is simply because it is an efficient way to work. If a colleague bumps my desk and my notepad shifts an inch to the left, I don't feel any burning, uncomfortable need to perfectly realign it to the edge of the table. It just doesn't bother me in the slightest. \n\nAnd as for counting other things... no, absolutely not. I don't count the books on a shelf, or the tiles on the floor, or the number of times I stir my tea. As I quite literally just finished explaining a moment ago, what brings me in here—the entire reason we are speaking today—is simply that I’ve gotten terribly stuck doing *these specific* little routines. It is the washing of my hands again after handling my wrist brace. It is the rechecking of the stove and the front door. And it is that silent counting I do through the steps before I leave the porch. But that is the *only* counting I do! \n\nAnd the whole reason it has become such a burden is that they’re starting to make me late for my shifts, even when I know perfectly well that I’ve already done enough. I know the door is locked, I know my hands are clean, but I get stuck anyway. That is the entire scope of the issue. There isn't any other arranging or symmetrical nonsense going on, I assure you.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had intrusive thoughts or felt the need to perform behaviors related to contamination, harm, or orderliness in other situations, even if only briefly or mildly?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... I must admit, I am finding this line of questioning incredibly circular. We are, quite frankly, beating a dead horse at this point, and I feel a bit as though I am standing at a lectern trying to get a rather inattentive freshman seminar to grasp a fundamental concept. \n\nLet me be as unequivocally clear as I can be, because it seems you are searching for some sprawling, generalized neurosis that simply does not exist. The answer is no, not in other situations. It is exactly, and only, what I have already laid out for you. \n\nI’ll be sitting peacefully, reading at home, minding my own business, or I'll be in the foyer getting ready for a museum shift... and that is when it happens. That ugly little thought keeps barging in, completely uninvited, insisting that I’ve left something unsafe, or that I’ve touched something terribly unclean, or that I have somehow put someone at risk. \n\nIt doesn't happen when I'm at the grocer, it doesn't happen when I'm taking a stroll through the park, and it doesn't happen when I'm actually at the museum cataloging documents. It happens right there, in those specific instances I just spent the better part of this appointment describing to you! And, as I have emphasized repeatedly, I know perfectly well it’s completely out of proportion. My faculties are entirely intact, Doctor. I know the thoughts are irrational. \n\nSo, no, there are no other situations, not even mildly. Can we perhaps accept that this is the full scope of the intrusion and finally move on to what we might actually do about it? Because this endless repetition of the same question is precisely the sort of inefficiency that drives me up the wall.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Because this endless repetition of the same question is precisely the sort of inefficiency that drives me up the wall.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had intrusive thoughts or felt the need to perform behaviors related to contamination, harm, or orderliness in other situations, even if only briefly or mildly?\n\nPatient: Doctor, please. I... I really must protest. Are you quite sure you are listening to me? Because you have just asked me the exact same question you asked mere moments ago. I mean, word for word, almost! It's as if I am speaking to a brick wall, or perhaps a recording that has gotten stuck on a rather unhelpful loop. \n\nI do not mean to be rude, but I am a man who values precision and efficiency, both in my work at the museum archives and in my daily life, and this... this circular conversation is becoming incredibly frustrating. I have already told you, in exhaustive detail, that these thoughts and behaviors—the hand washing, the stove checking, the porch counting—are entirely confined to those specific moments I described. \n\nI do not have these issues when I am out running errands. I do not have them when I am visiting with my neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, though heaven knows her house could use a good dusting. I do not have them when I am carefully handling a fragile, two-hundred-year-old letter at work. It is only in those particular situations at home, getting ready to leave, that this ridiculous, irrational fear of contamination or harm rears its ugly head. \n\nI have made it abundantly clear that I know these thoughts are nonsensical. I know I am not going to cause a disaster if I don't check the deadbolt for a fifth time. But I get stuck anyway. That is the whole problem! And I have told you, unequivocally, that there are no other situations where this happens. Not briefly, not mildly, not at all. \n\nSo, I ask you again, with all due respect: can we please, for the love of all that is holy, move past this endless interrogation about things I do not experience, and actually discuss how I might go about fixing the very specific problem I came here for? Because I am losing precious time here, Doctor, and as I mentioned, punctuality is something I take very seriously."
